Reed warblers nest among them, perfectly at home.

One of the birds lands on Miketsu's shoulder, singing sweetly and brushing its beak against her cheek now and then.

But beneath the warbler's lovely song, another sound seems to linger...

"It sounds like... someone's crying."


<poem> A little reed spirit crouches by the shore. Reed flowers blown by the wind land softly in her hair, drifting like glowing feathers under the summer sun. But the sad little spirit doesn't notice any of it—she just hides her face and quietly cries. Miketsu doesn't walk up right away. Gentle and attentive, she stays hidden in the reeds behind the spirit, silently wondering what has made her so sad. The spirit looks down at her reflection in the water and her plain white clothes, feeling like she's neither as bright and lovely as a lotus nor as tall and powerful as an ancient tree. "If only I weren't so ordinary... I wish I could be colorful and special like them." She whispers her troubles to her shadow, and little by little, she calms down. Just as she's about to stand, a fox's reflection appears in the water, and she turns around in surprise. "That's what you should do-speak your worries out loud. Do you feel a bit better now?" Miketsu steps out from the reeds, sits beside her, and places a water pouch into her hands. "Lady Miketsu... you heard everything?" "Do you think my worries are... kind of silly?" "It's not silly to have a wish or to want to become something more. "I once met a little spirit who wished to become a god-someone others needed and were grateful for." "She had no divine powers, but she answered prayers with her own effort. In the end, she became a goddess in the hearts of those she protected." "There was also a god who wished for fate to follow his own choices." "And because of that, he was never trapped by destiny." The gentle goddess looks off into the distance and softly shares the stories of those from long ago. "Maybe what defines who we are isn't what we're born with-but our dreams, and how hard we try to reach them."

<poem>

The little spirit listens closely to the story. In the distance, the water, sky, and terraced fields blend together in soft, beautiful colors, and her mood slowly becomes bright and colorful too.

As Miketsu looks at the reeds swaying by the lake, their colors connecting sky and earth, then at the reed spirit in front of her dressed in all white, she suddenly feels inspired.

"You see the beauty in the colors of the world, and you hope to be just as full of color and life."

"So, would you like to wear the sky and earth as your clothes?"

Hearing Miketsu's question, the little spirit's eyes show both surprise and confusion. She looks down at her plain attire, hesitating.

"All the colors in the world... but I'm just... Can I really?"

"As long as you can feel the colors of the world, you can take them in as your own."

Miketsu waves her hand, stirring a soft breeze that lifts the reed flowers into the air. They swirl around and float across the sky in all directions.

"Say out loud the colors you feel. The reed flowers will hear you." The little spirit thinks for a moment, then slowly begins to describe her feelings about colors.

"The petals flying into the sky will meet a soft sky blue-that's my favorite. It always feels calm and free, and just seeing it puts me at ease."

"The ones that fall into the water pick up a pale green, and when the sun hits the waves, they sparkle with gold. Just one glance makes me feel like I'm drifting along with those colors."

As they pass through the terraced fields, they fill up with endless shades o

green. Each one feels different...The reed spirit speaks with such focus that she doesn't even notice how the reed petals are now circling them, glowing with many colors and dyeing her once-plain clothes.

"Some petals, no matter where they go, stay pure white and keep their own shape."

The white petals gently land on them, adding a final touch to their clothes.

In that moment, the two of them shine in the sunlight, as if wrapped in cloaks made of feathers.

"It's... so beautiful."

Dressed in her colorful clothes, the reed spirit gazes at her reflection in the water. Then, unable to hold back, she begins to dance with the wind. Her figure by the water blends perfectly with the vast fields beyond. Her bright and joyful dance is a gift in return to the land. Miketsu watches it all with a thoughtful gaze.

"Maybe... this too is part of what a harvest means."


Without realizing it, dusk arrives. Everything is covered in a warm orange-red

The bright and colorful sights from earlier slowly melt into the setting sun, then gently fade away.

The little spirit finally snaps out of her endless dance. She looks back and sees Miketsu resting in the reeds, already fast asleep on a freshly wove reed mat.

The last light of the sunset shines through the reeds, casting soft, dappled shadows across the gentle goddess's face.

Miketsu is always like this—used to calm and comfort, and to the warmth and softness of the land

The little spirit takes reed roots and stems to make a doll, then wraps it in a pretty robe made from early golden leaves.

She gently places the gift into Miketsu's hands.

"Lady Miketsu, your favorite color must be the golden color of harvest."

"Your wishes are always so simple and warm."

She touches the cool reed mat, then curls up beside the kind goddess, closing her eyes with the sunset, full of peace.

Miketsu may not be the strongest or the wisest goddess, but her wish stays firm and never changes.

Night quietly falls. From a distant tree, a figure silently watches the scene unfold.

"Even the moonlight tonight feels so gentle."

This is the Talisman Pass for July. The focus of the event was Shinyuu Inugami.

The limited rewards for the June Talisman Pass are the Plum Blizzard skin, Wandering Guest illustration, World Visitor illustration, and Jade Frost frame.


As the blood-red sunset bathed the plum grove, a wandering swordsman with an unmarked blade at his waist stepped into a village abandoned by the Plum Deity. There, beneath a withered tree, he met a lonely girl with eyes like dried buds. It felt like fate. Guided by her, the wanderer ventured alone into a dark lair despised by the gods...

—Tales of the Plum Wanderer: Volume One

Inugami held up a lantern, reading the first chapter of his new wanderer tale again and again, completely satisfied. Just then, Sparrow tapped lightly on the window-three short taps, one long. It was the signal he and his partner had agreed on.

Inugami closed the scroll and slipped silently out the window. By the time he landed, he had already returned to his canine form. Sparrow touched beak to nose with him. "Mr. Inugami, the route's clear. Let's go!"

Three days earlier, Inugami had arrived in this village, famous for its plum blossoms and dog performances. It was Dragon Plum season-the flowers were blooming like pink clouds, and the drooping branches looked like waterfalls. Visitors were everywhere. But at the village center stood a giant plum tree, nearly twice as tall as the others, completely bare and blackened.

Not even a tiny bud had formed. People were talking about it nonstop.

“That tree's cursed by the gods. That's why it won't bloom." Someone tugged at Inugami's sleeve. He looked down and saw a little girl, barely reaching his tail, her face and clothes covered in dirt. "Sir, your sword looks really powerful. Can you help me?"

The girl's name was Mena. She had no parents and lived with a little dog named Bone. Not long ago, Bone was taken by a group called the Divine Canine Society and forced to become a performing dog. Mena spotted him during a show but was quickly chased out by the trainers. She said the Society puts on shows for the Plum Deity during Dragon Plum season to pray for protection over the village, but in truth, they abuse the dogs and trap them with demonic contracts so they can't escape. That's why the deity turned away, and the tree stopped blooming.

Inugami carried Mena on his shoulders and asked around the village, but no one seemed to know her. She kept hiding her head under his hat and wouldn't say where she came from. With no other leads, Inugami decided to sneak into the Society and take a look for himself.

Now, he and the sparrow were already inside the Divine Canine Society's base. Guided by Sparrow, Inugami ran swiftly through the moonlight with his partner on his back. Because of a technique he had practiced, his fur had turned pure white, giving him a noble, masterly look that stood out among the other dogs. That's how he caught the troupe's eye and was chosen as a top performer with the best treatment.

"I'm going to save Mena's dog—and every innocent performing dog here!" At the end of their midnight search, the wanderer saw rows of tiny, stinking cages like pigeon coops. He let out a long, angry howl toward the moon and swore to make things right.


When the village's plum trees reach full bloom, the grand Divine Canine Parade takes place. Training within the Divine Canine Society becomes especially harsh during this time.

The parade is a ritual dance to pray for blessings from the Plum Spirit, and the most skilled and beautiful performing dogs are chosen to play the role of the Plum Attendant. As a seasoned shikigami, Inugami stood out and earned the role.

"Mr. Inugami is truly a mature soul now—so adaptable," Sparrow sighed. In past selections, Inugami faced trials unlike any he'd seen in battle: strumming a samisen with his tail, balancing a fragile hot spring egg on his head while crossing a flaming beam, or dancing a welcome jig in a frilly, bow-tied dog costume...

It all sounded ridiculous, but only Inugami could pull it off perfectly and avoid punishment. Many dogs collapsed under the pressure. The Society refused to help, letting sick performers rot in cages. If a dog slacked off, it was beaten black and blue.

Thankfully, the wanderer operation at night was going smoothly. Inugami had learned the condition of every performer and set a signal for their escape on parade day. Mena secretly met with him, bringing herbs she'd somehow found and asking him to pass them on to the injured dogs.

That night, Mena lay on the Society wall and saw the familiar white dog run beneath the blooming plums. When he emerged, he was in full wanderer attire.

She handed him the herbs as usual, but this time she noticed a deep cane mark on his body.

It had happened that day. Bone had made a mistake during training and was taken for punishment right in front of Inugami. Furious, Inugami barked and threw himself over Bone as the first blow fell. The trainers didn't dare harm him, so they let it go.

Mena burst into tears. "I'm sorry, Big Dog Brother... I'm so sorry... sniff...

thank you..."

"Shh, it's okay," Inugami said, awkwardly patting her back. "For a battle-hardened wanderer, this is nothing. What matters is that you and Bone have always been together. No one should be left behind. Every pup deserves to go home safe, and stay with the ones who love them."


On the day of the Divine Canine Parade, just after sunset, lanterns lit up the® stage in the plum grove, casting a glow over the deep blue sky.

Visitors arrived in droves, chatting excitedly about the upcoming performance.

Suddenly, the sharp sound of a samisen rang out like pearls falling on jade.

The crowd fell silent and looked toward the stage. Several performance dogs sat at the back, strumming the samisen with their tails or ringing divine bells in their mouths. Painted with plum patterns, they barked in rhythm with the music as they escorted a palanquin decorated with plum blossoms to the front.

Atop it sat a snow-white dog, proud and dignified, wearing a divine mask, his fur catching the moonlight. The music swelled as the dogs danced around the Plum Attendant, their movements making the plum patterns on their fur ripple like blooming waves.

The music peaked like a sudden clap of thunder. As the plum waves faded, the dogs stepped aside, and the audience gasped—the divine attendant had vanished. In his place was a cloaked wanderer in plum robes, seated on the palanquin. A wide-brimmed hat hid Inugami's face, his sleeves fluttering in the wind.

Under the moonlight, the wanderer placed a hand on his blade and slowly rose. The samisen chimed again, and a white blade flashed from its sheath.

With a long cry, the wanderer danced, his swordplay divine.

"That was amazing, Mr. Inugami! Don't stop now!" The sparrow flew overhead, its feathers slicing through the ropes that bound the dogs.

Meanwhile, a young girl's voice joined the music—it was Mena, singing of her longing for Bone from her perch in the plum tree.

The crowd broke into chaos. Trainers rushed forward but were frozen in*" place by the wanderer's icy spells. Clearing his throat, Inugami pulled a scroll from his sleeve—his latest work, Tales of the Plum Wanderer—and read it aloud, exposing the crimes of the Divine Canine Society.

"In just a few days, the early plum blossoms reached full bloom. But in those same few days, several puppies died at the hands of this heartless, twisted group. They'll never return home to see the blossoms with those who love them."

Outraged, the crowd hurled things at the trainers. The Society was forced to break all demonic contracts on the dogs. The audience cleared a path, and the freed dogs leapt over the trainers, barking joyfully as they ran into the moonlight and the sea of plum blossoms—their road home.

Inugami turned to the plum tree—Mena, who had been there, was gone.


=== Fragrant Waterfall

"And then?" Seimei took a small sip of his honeyed wine, smiling. They were sitting beneath a weeping plum tree on the outskirts of Heian-Kyo, drinking together to welcome a returning wanderer.

"And then the dragon plum really bloomed! Oh, Master Seimei, you wouldn't believe it... overnight, that withered tree burst into radiant blossoms, as if a god had lightly tapped it with a flowering branch. A fragrant waterfall poured down from the heavens... All other plum trees paled in comparison. The whole village was wrapped in plum fragrance—it was so, so beautiful."

Inugami waved his hands, laughing heartily. His tail wagged wildly, stirring fallen petals everywhere.

On the day the dragon plum bloomed, the villagers woke to its scent, intoxicated by its sweetness. They pushed aside curtain after curtain of blossoms and stepped into a dreamlike scene-two small dogs, covered in petals, sleeping under the tree with heads resting on each other.

The villagers held their breath. This must be a divine sign, they whispered.

The puppies, startled by footsteps, shook off the petals and walked toward them.

The black one they knew-Bone, rescued by Inugami from the Divine Canine Society. The brown one was a stranger, never seen before. It barely responded no matter how they called, until someone mentioned Mena. Then it nuzzled and bumped that person insistently-so they named it "Mena."

These two furry creatures seemed so close, as if they'd relied on each other forever.

Seimei gently rubbed his cupa gift from Hiromasa, decorated with a painted dragon plum waterfall. Upon hearing the name "Mena," he raised an eyebrow, then smiled warmly.

"I see. That's truly wonderful," said Seimei.

"Truly wonderful," echoed Inugami with a nod.

After a pause, Inugami added, "The villagers saw the divine dogs beneath the tree as a good omen. The 'Tales of the Plum Wanderer' sold well. They used the money to restore the shrine to the Plum Deity and built splendid kennels for Mena and Bone. And it seems they really do have some divine power.

Wounds licked by them heal quickly, and elders feel less pain... Everyone loves those two friendly pups. They're given so much meat they can't even finish it."

After a pause, Inugami added, "The villagers saw the divine dogs beneath the tree as a good omen. The 'Tales of the Plum Wanderer' sold well. They used the money to restore the shrine to the Plum Deity and built splendid kennels for Mena and Bone. And it seems they really do have some divine power.

Wounds licked by them heal quickly, and elders feel less pain... Everyone loves those two friendly pups. They're given so much meat they can't even finish it."

As the tale ended, a gentle breeze stirred. Plum petals fell like snow beneath the tree. Seimei and Inugami smiled and raised their cups again in a quiet toast.

This is the Talisman Paas for August. The focus of this event was Karuta.

The limited rewards for the August Talisman Pass are the Starry Blossom skin, Moonlit Blooms illustration, Cards' Secrets illustration, and Flowermoon Dream frame.




"No way, no way, there's no chance I can beat you!"

"Master Karuta, please show mercy and let me win just this once. Maybe I won't even notice!"

"Hmm-hmm."

Karuta, master of Cabert and Cards, sat cheerfully on the table, lips curved like a kitten as she eyed the sulking girl in front of her.

The girl's rosy face and jingling hairpins were livelier than the tune on stage.

Karuta idly twirled a lock of the girl's hair.

"It's not that I refuse to let you win," she said, hopping lightly off the table and stretching.

"If victory is handed to you, you'll forget how precious these memories are."

"Isn't there a saying? Only what you work hard for stays clear in your mind."

"I remember, I remember everything!" the girl cried, lunging for a hug.

Karuta leaned back as Hanafuda Scenery unfurled behind her. The girl caught only air; by the time she steadied herself, the mirage had vanished, leaving Karuta's laughter lingering.

Strolling through Hanafuda Scenery, Karuta expected the scenery to fade as memories returned, yet real landscapes from the cards quietly filled the empty spaces.

Under a willow, something unusual caught her eye.

A hanafuda card, much smaller than normal, hung in the air like a leaf.

Its face held no image or text, only shifting pastel hues.

"So many secrets hidden here..." she mused.

A gentle touch sent her plunging into memories she had never seen.


When Karuta opened her eyes, it was a warm afternoon, and someone was calling her name from below.

"...Sis... Sis..."

She recognized the familiar voice and sat up, almost slipping off the tree branch she had been resting on. Steadying herself, she looked down-and sure enough, it was her younger brother.

"What... happened?" she murmured, still dazed.

"Sis! Look!" Her brother held up a small basin, sweating so much his hair stuck to his forehead, but he didn't mind at all—his face lit up with a bright smile.

Inside the basin, a single tadpole swam alone.

Karuta's heart gave a jolt. It was just an ordinary afternoon from a distant memory.

A plain, everyday moment—yet it brought tears to her eyes.

Her brother's voice pulled her back from her thoughts.

Karuta flipped down and landed lightly. An orange blur leapt after her, stepping squarely on her shoulder before touching down. Its orange tail curled high as the cat stretched lazily.

"Ginger!" Her brother quickly shielded the basin. "You almost jumped into it!"

"Meow—!" Ginger shook its head and strolled off toward home.

"You caught just one tadpole after all that effort, and now you're worried Ginger will knock it over?" Karuta tapped her brother's forehead.

"Come on, let's go to the pond."

"You'll help me catch more?" His eyes sparkled.

"I'm helping you release this poor little guy, then we're washing your face and heading home!"

"Aww...

The sun hadn't fully set, but the lights were already on in their cozy little house. The warm glow carried the scent of dinner.

Karuta pushed open the gate, steadying her emotions, and sure enough, the scene was just as she remembered. Her father was tending the flowerbeds, and through the lattice window, her mother moved busily in the kitchen.

She ran inside without even taking off her shoes and hugged her mother tightly from behind.

"Mom..."

"What is it, dear?" Her mother's voice was gentle. Her hands didn't stop moving as she added Karuta's favorite dish into the simmering pot. Wrapped in the rising steam, she turned around and embraced Karuta.

No words were needed. A mother's arms had always been her safe haven.

"My little Karuta... Did something happen out there?"

...No." After a while, Karuta looked up, pretending everything was fine. Right now, she didn't want to disturb this memory-she just wanted to savor it.

"There, there." A pair of broad hands patted Karuta's head—it was her father.

He tucked a small blue flower into her hair.

"Don't cling to your mother. Come help me set the table. After dinner, you two still need to help me turn the soil."

"Huh? Again?" her brother grumbled, but when he saw their father raise a fist in mock warning, his tone changed immediately. "Tilling? I love tilling! Let's

Her mother gently pinched Karuta's cheek. "Alright, dinner first. I made all your favorite dishes today."

"Mm..." Karuta answered softly.

But before her words had fully settled, the figures around her suddenly dissolved into swirling colors.

The colors swept past her eyes. When she opened them again, she was back on the same tree branch, and her brother's voice rang out from below once more.

"What... is this...?"


This was only a fleeting shard of memory-something that couldn't be extended, endlessly repeating the same cycle.

Karuta held tightly to that warmth, not knowing how many times she had experienced the same dream.

Eventually, she began trying to change how her family acted, hoping that different choices or behaviors might stretch the limits of the memory.

But the answer was clear: no. Memories are like events set in stone-what has already happened cannot be changed.

Over time, her joy gave way to exhaustion, and exhaustion turned to sorrow.

Her efforts were like trying to scoop the moon out of water—so near to her eyes, yet always out of reach.

"Then... what should I do?"

After another failed attempt, Karuta sat quietly, waiting for everything to begin again. A rare look of loneliness and sadness appeared on her face.

"If this is how it ends, maybe... it would've been better if l'd never come across that card at all?"

But this time, when her mother gently cupped Karuta's cheeks again, something had changed.

It was as if a character from a painting had suddenly come to life-her mother's gaze broke free of its limits, crossing time itself. There was a depth in her eyes that had never been there before.

"Go and do the things you've always wanted to do but never had the chance to."

"The things l've always wanted to do... but never had the chance to?"

The memory burst into color and rushed toward Karuta.

The swirling colors blurred her vision, but inside, everything was growing clearer and clearer.


In the afternoon, her younger brother's voice called out to her once more.

Karuta climbed down from the tree with practiced ease and caught Ginger mid-air as the orange cat leapt down alongside her.

Karuta buried her face in Ginger's soft fur. The cat gave an annoyed meow.

"I'll remember every step you taught me on the roof beams.

"Goodbye, Ginger."

She set Ginger down. Its tail curled high as it brushed fondly against her ankle, then its figure slowly faded from view.

By the pond, Karuta carefully wiped her brother's face clean, then held out her pinky finger.

"Why a pinky promise all of a sudden?" her brother asked, puzzled.

"Just do it."

"Promise me—no matter how far apart we are, you'll find me and come back to me."

"But I'm right here!"

"Say it anyway."

"No matter where you are, or how far, I'll always find you, Sis."

"Mm... Pinky swear! Cross my heart, hope to die." His grin lit up his whole face.

"Goodbye, little brother." Karuta hugged him tightly.

Karuta was left alone by the pond.

"Mother..."

Karut no longer tried to change anything. Just like when she first entered the memory, she gently hugged her mother from behind

"So much has happened since then. I wanted to tell you everything."

"But for now, I just want you to know that I'm doing well. You don't have to worry anymore."

Her mother paused for a moment, then continued stirring the food in the pot.

"Silly girl. Of course I know. No matter what happens, you'll be just fine."

A broader arm wrapped around them both as her father joined the embrace.

"Say what's in your heart, child," came her father's voice from above.

"We'll meet again one day. But before then—“

"Live your life well."

"Father. Mother."

"Goodbye."

The warmth of their embrace slowly faded. The world returned to its swirl of soft, colorful light, brushing gently across Karuta's cheeks and wiping away her tears.

When she opened her eyes again, Karuta was back in Hanafuda Scenery.

A small hanafuda card floated before her. The messy colors on its surface had settled into a warm scene of a family of four gathered around a dinner table.

So that was it.

This was what she had always wanted to do, but never got the chance— to say a proper goodbye to her family.

Even if it was only in a memory...

Karuta let out a soft sigh. Her heart felt lighter.

She picked up the hanafuda card and turned it over-sure enough, there was a familiar butterfly mark in one corner.

It must have been a little Easter egg left behind in Hanafuda Scenery by someone from her past. Maybe they were afraid she'd forget everything and lose herself, so they left these fragments behind, hoping she might one day find them again.

But back then, she'd been too focused on winning games to notice these small, quiet cards.

Karuta carefully tucked the card into her robe.

A thought rose quietly in her mind-maybe there were more of these hidden throughout "Hanafuda Hyakkei."

At the thought, a spark of excitement lit up in her eyes.

It seemed that besides running Cabaret and Cards and waiting for challengers to play hanafuda, she now had one more adventure to look forward to.

This is the Talisman Pass for September. The focus of this event was Shura Kidoumaru.

The limited rewards for the September Talisman Pass are the Inky Waves skin, Carp Race illustration, Ghost Flower paid illustration and Rolling Banners frame.




When Kidomaru tracked down the seventeenth demon, he noticed something off in the air. He stepped onto a rooftop and saw an empty bamboo pole standing by a house=today was the festival, and carp banners should have been fluttering here.

But now, only the sound of children crying cut through the rain.

"What a pathetic display," Kidomaru muttered from above, watching the two children on the altar.

The villagers holding torches were shaking, the firelight casting shadows across the clan leader's wife's hollow eyes. In her arms, she held a freshly washed carp banner that hadn't yet been hung.

"We offer these two children to the gods, praying they will keep our clan safe...' the clan leader's voice trembled as thunder cracked through his prayer.

Behind the altar, the pond suddenly churned, and a moss-covered demon rose from the water, the scales on its forehead glinting with a strange light.

Kidomaru narrowed his eyes. It seemed this clan had been threatened by the demon and was offering up the children to ensure their safety. Pathetic.

"No, please..." The clan leader's wife finally snapped out of her daze.

Clutching the fabric tightly, she sobbed, "Why does it have to be these two... they're still so young!"

"As the clan leader, how could I let others carry this burden?!"

The man turned and held back his wife, grabbing the blade from her hands.

"If two lives can save a hundred, then I must make that choice as leader." Something in those words triggered a memory in Kidomaru. He let out a cold snort. "Self-righteous fool."


The ritual seemed ready to begin.

The demon leapt from the water again, just about to devour the two children, when the clan leader's wife, eyes wide with fury, drew her husband's sword and rushed forward to strike the demon down.

But before she could act, her husband stopped her and took the blade from her hands.

The demon growled in frustration, and the clan leader knelt before it alongside the other villagers.

"All this, in the name of so-called peace-sacrificing their own flesh and blood..." Kidomaru frowned.

The water erupted once more, and the demon was finally about to reach the children—

Kidomaru's chains moved faster than thought, but froze midair. He saw the clan leader reverse the blade and stab himself, letting blood flow through the grooves of the hilt and into the cracks of the altar.

Large incantation marks lit up on the ground. Kidomaru's eyes narrowed-this man had buried a sealing circle beneath the altar.

The demon's body was trapped, unable to move for the moment.

For some reason, Kidomaru suddenly remembered his teacher's blurry face back in the demon city.

What a pity. The demon before him was far too powerful. Despite the carefully prepared circle, which had been years in the making, it still wasn't enough to destroy it.


The clan leader had turned pale from blood loss, and the eastern circle center was beginning to loosen. The demon was about to break free when Kidomaru raised his chains.

But for some reason, the demon froze. All the villagers present had placed their burned hands on the edge of the circle, sealing the final gap.

Kidomaru instinctively licked his lower lip. "Of course... it's in moments like this that people's choices become interesting."

The sound of chains whipping through the air erupted at the same time as thunder.

Kidomaru danced in the storm. The instant the chains pierced the demon, he saw an image deep within its scales—a young version of the clan leader, clenching his fists as his sister was dragged into the pond, on this same day years ago.

"Thank you... d-divine one..." The villagers seemed to mistake the blood-soaked Kidomaru for a god.

The title was far too ironic. Kidomaru let out a cold laugh, then devoured the demon's remains in full view of the crowd. The burning, filthy blood gave him a strange sense of peace.

The villagers were struck silent by what they saw. Only the two rescued children tilted their heads in confusion.


To show their appreciation, the clan leader and his wife gave Kidomaru a newly embroidered yukata, with a beautiful carp banner stitched at the hem.

"You still look quite young, so we picked this pattern, hoping it brings good fortune..."

Kidomaru caught a faint scent of mugwort on the fabric, and suddenly remembered how, many years ago, his teacher once hung carp banners outside the house for him and Seimei.

Back then, he found it silly and troublesome, but now, he reached out and accepted the yukata.

Kidomaru crouched once more on the rooftop. In the distant marketplace, children in bright new outfits were heading to the festival, praying for health and peace.

Along with the new clothes, the clan leader had also given him a piece of Chitose candy, just like in the past. A faint sweetness now came from the pocket inside his clothes.

A brand-new yukata, warm festival lights, and delicious Chitose candy.

"I used to think this stuff was ridiculous..." Kidomaru tugged at the collar, listening to the laughter drifting from the crowd.

As the breeze passed by, the sleeves of the yukata fluttered like real carp banners swimming through the twilight sky.

This is the Talisman Pass for October. The focus of this event was Nekogawa.

The limited rewards for the October Talisman Pass are the Snowthaw Bloom skin, Blossom Glow illustration, Old Bond illustration, and Crimson Snow frame.


When heavy snow falls, hot springs become especially popular. Watching the snow while soaking in the springs has always been a favorite pastime for the nobles of Heian-Kyo. But with the snowfall these past two days being so heavy, reaching the Nekogawa Annex halfway up the mountain has become rather difficult. I could use my powers to clear the snow along the way, but that would spoil the atmosphere. And with so few visitors lately, keeping the place open would be a waste of effort. The only ones likely to come in this weather are Minamoto clan heirs carrying vouchers signed by the family head.

That means no profit at all, only losses. After thinking it over, I decided to simply lock the doors and enjoy a rare, quiet holiday instead.

Not long ago, the red plum blossoms in the courtyard had bloomed beautifully. To suit the season, Koban dressed himself in a new outfit— its bright festive colors stand out against the white snow. He looked like a round little piggy bank that made you want to toss a few coins into him. He even ordered me to change into winter clothes to improve the place's overall image. When it comes to things like this, he is always the most enthusiastic.

So when I mentioned closing the place, he was the only one who objected, muttering, "Now no one will see this look. All my effort has gone to waste."

Magnate, sipping tea nearby, said calmly, "The outfit isn't the real issue. What you're worried about is that no one will drop money into you, piggy bank."

Koban changed back into his true form, baring his claws and teeth. Whether you call it clever or needless, his look had changed too. He no longer resembled flowing water but something between snow and cloud. Magnate let out a puff of white breath and turned away, treating him as if he were nothing more than snow bending the villa's branches, just part of the winter scenery. Koban, who had long since lost any dignity, didn't mind much. He soon turned back into his piggy bank form, rolling around on the ground and pestering me to come up with something fun to make it up to him.

I said, "Have you heard of the famous Hanafuda Hall on the Isle of Sorrow?

As it happens, I have a set of hanafuda cards here. Let's play a game." Koban said, "That sounds good. But I won't play with you. I'll be the dealer. The dealer takes it all."

Magnate sighed. "There's no one else here, so that leaves only me, doesn't it?"

"You're forgetting something," I told him. "Besides you two, there are plenty of other cats here."

"...You mean people, right? And where would they get the money to trade for chips?"

"We're closed today," I said. "We can afford to enjoy a touch of elegance once in a while.”

"No!" Koban shot back. "I don't care about elegance. If you want to start a game, put in your coins first. That's the rule!"


We gave Koban some dignity by tossing a coin into the piggy bank, just as it asked. The coin jingled whenever Koban moved. It was clearly delighted by the sound, and it did carry a touch of good fortune.

"Put it on their tab," I said.

Magnate added, "Once the snow on the mountain path melts a bit, let Koban stay in piggy bank form. We might even break a sales record."

The first to join the card game was a cat with an honest, simple look.

I put in the coin for him and turned him back into a man. He looked lost, unsure why he was even here. When he had come to Nekogawa Annex it felt like summer, but now it was already the middle of winter.

"Did he owe you that much? Working for free this long without turning back?" Magnate asked.

"Not exactly," I replied. "When he first arrived, I heard his thoughts. He used to make a living writing stories, but misfortune after misfortune left him believing his career was over. He planned to turn to theft. Being clever, he spent his last savings on a Nekogawa Annex ticket, intending to steal big once and retire for good."

Koban said, "Thieves don't quit that easily."

"So to keep the guests safe, and to stop him from falling further, I turned him into a cat for the time being and kept him here. I've read his stories—he's got talent. If a famous publisher noticed him one day, he might become a great writer. Until then, a few days as a cat here isn't the worst fate," I said.

"Not just a few days," Magnate muttered.

The story-writing cat was gentle by nature and not very active. When guests scratched his chin, he'd fall into deep thought. Maybe he still clung to human habits, since he'd sneak treats from guests now and then. They only thought he was clever, and he even became one of Nekogawa Annex's star employees.

The man and I sat across from each other for a long while before he finally asked, "What's going on?"

"It's snowing outside," I said.

"Since we've got nothing better to do, why not

play a round of Hanafuda?"

The writer was far more refined and elegant than us pretentious merchants.

He was better at Hanafuda, and luck was on his side. I lost the first round, so we switched to the best of three. We ended up playing all five rounds, and he still won.

"Alright, you win," I said. "Make a wish."

"I wish more people could read my stories," he answered.

I nodded and turned him back into a cat.

Magnate gave me a look, half speechless, as I sipped my tea and smiled. "I never said l'd grant it. I only wanted to see what he'd wish for. His dream is already within reach—he just needs patience. At least now we know he cares less about stealing, more about his goal. And for that coin we have Koban? He’ll have to work it off.”


The second to join the card game was a child. Even as a cat she stayed tiny, so most guests hardly ever saw her. When they did, they’d gasp. “There’s such a small cat here! Was this kitten born in the annex?”

Not at all.

The child had been left behind in the annex. Yes, even here that sort of thing happens. The couple who brought her left without her. They looked respectable on the surface, but they were drowning in gambling debts and desperate to cast off the burden. They comforted themselves by thinking the wealthy patrons of the annex would give her a fine future.

I knew their secret from the start, but I figured the child might be better off not going back. Besides, we'd never had such a little kitten here before—it was something new. So I acted like I hadn't noticed. Later, I sold their secret to a spirit who cared about such matters. What became of them after that, I don't know.

Another reason I turned her into a cat was simple: kittens are much easier to care for than children. Every cat in the annex looked after her. Not everyone would do the same for a child. Now that she's back in human form, we've had to teach her hanafuda from scratch.

"I know this," she said.

She didn't. Not at all.

I worried she'd cry if she lost and ruin our rare day off, so I tried my hardest to let her win. But losing to someone who doesn't even know the rules is hard—like trying to trade with someone who doesn't understand money.

Luckily, when money fails, barter works. So I pretended to play along with her made-up rules.

She won, delighted. I told her, "You can make a wish too."

"I don't have one," she said. "I don't think I want anything right now. Can we play hanafuda again?"

"No," I said, and turned her back into a kitten. She hopped onto the table, scattering the cards everywhere. I'm sure it was an accident.

Magnate laughed at me, Koban too, until the kitten pounced on the fat piggy bank and knocked it headfirst into a fluffy snowdrift. After a bright jingle, she scampered off again.


We ended up playing cards with a bunch of cats-or really, people I'd turned into cats. Everyone had a different reason for being here, but what they all had in common was that one day they stepped into Nekogawa Annex and, in the mist of the hot springs, spilled their secrets to me.

Some had done bad things and I caught them. I didn't feel right just letting them walk away, so I kept them around until I could sell their secrets to someone who'd deal with it. Some were stuck with problems and had no way out, so turning into a cat gave them a break to clear their heads. And some just overspent at Nekogawa Annex and had to work off their debts as staff. I don't blame them. I've spent ages figuring out how to make guests hand me their money willingly. That's the fun of business.

As for betting chips and leaving it up to fate, that's not really a trade, so it's less exciting. But I still love the game itself, especially on a rare day off.

That day we had wins and losses. I heard plenty of wishes, but I didn't grant a single one. Magnate refused at first, but in the end couldn't resist and joined in. Halfway through, Tesso and Jikikaeru showed up to see if they could reclaim contraband the Heian Merchant Guild had confiscated, and of course we sat them down for a few rounds too.

The Koban piggy bank kept filling up until it was so heavy it couldn't even roll.

Night fell. Lanterns glowed along the corridor. I put on a serious face and told the piggy bank, "You're stuffed full of Coins. Time to smash you open."

With a loud yelp, the piggy bank turned back into a big cat. Coins clattered everywhere, many dropping into the hot spring and shimmering gold beneath the ripples. A breeze stirred. Snow fell from the branches and made the red plum blossoms stand out even brighter. Nekogawa Annex's cats crowded close together, shoulder to shoulder. It was one of those rare, perfect winter moments.

"Will the snow melt tomorrow?" Magnate asked.

"I don't know," I said. "I hope it melts soon, and I hope it never does."

Either way, I've got money and l've got cats. That's more than enough for an ideal life, don't you think?

This is the Talisman Pass for November. The focus of this event was Seishi Hiyoribou.

The limited rewards for the November Talisman Pass are the Windbracing Sun skin, Sweet Lumen illustration, Luminary Lake illustration, and Starry Sweetdream frame.


The rain had stopped unnoticed, yet the humid air hung like a suffocating net.

Hiyoribou shivered as she traversed the bamboo path. The courtyard hidden deep within the grove resembled an ukiyo-e masterpiece - raked gravel gardens with mossy stones, vermilion-lacquered corridors, and... the dolls suspended from the eaves.

Normally, sunshine dolls should occupy these positions.

"Are these dolls... crying?" she murmured.

Each exquisitely crafted face bore identical sorrow - teardrops pooled in downcast eyes, lips pressed into downward arcs, their lavish garments hanging ponderously.

After prolonged observation, Hiyoribou suddenly looked skyward. Stranger still, while sunlight bathed the world beyond, the courtyard remained shrouded in gloom.

She attempted summoning sunlight repeatedly until interrupted by a rasping voice.

"It's no use."

A white-haired elder emerged from the shadows, with sleepless eyes. His parchment-like voice accompanied woodcarving motions:

"Sunlight cannot pierce this place."


After uttering these words, the old man fell silent, yet his gaze kept drifting toward the sorrowful dolls beneath the eaves.

Hiyoribou cautiously settled on the steps, observing him. She noticed that whenever his clouded eyes lingered on a particular doll, the grief etched between its brows deepened imperceptibly, causing ripples in the courtyard's intangible heaviness.

"So that's how it is," she realized. "These dolls are vessels containing his sorrow."

From that day onward, Hiyoribou visited the lonely Courtyard daily. She gently brushed dust from the dolls' sleeves as if polishing fragile treasures. Swept fallen leaves into miniature mounds. Silently brewed tea beside the old man, its rising steam their only warmth.

More often, she simply sat quietly, her presence clinging like tenacious twilight to this ash-gray world.

Then one day, as the old man stared at a small doll with coiled hair and particularly mournful eyes, he spoke in a voice rasping with decades of silence:

"My daughter... was your age when she left."

The old man convulsed with sudden coughs, his floodgates creaking open to release a black torrent of buried memories.

"The sunlight was rare that day. She went out with friends. There'd been Spirit sightings nearby...I should've gone with her."

"But I indulged in the precious sunshine... fell asleep right there." 

"I could've... saved her..." His voice broke into voiceless weeping. For years he'd guarded this Courtyard, pouring every unshed tear and 

bone-gnawing agony into these dolls - letting them weep where he could not. Ever since, sunlight became forbidden here, the Courtyard transformed into a coffin for his colossal grief.


Hiyoribou listened quietly, feeling the weight of each word sinking into her heart.

She didn't speak immediately, but gently approached and placed her palm over the old man's icy hand.

"I understand..." she said with the purest emotional perception of a vessel spirit, though somewhat awkwardly, "That sorrow when someone important leaves... it can't be forgotten."

"We can't erase this grief, but perhaps... we can let the light in."

The old man curled up, his gaunt shoulders shaking violently. Hiyoribou wrapped her small arms around his trembling body, her slight shoulders attempting to bear this tsunami-like sorrow.

"I used to fear many things too. But see," she whispered tenderly, gazing at the barely noticeable sprout in the courtyard's brick crevice, "Life persists stubbornly even where light can't reach."

Tears finally fell as the old man's rigid posture gradually relaxed. His eyes, when looking at the doll in his hands, no longer held only anguish - distant tenderness and acceptance now surfaced.

When unspeakable regrets dissolved into long, trembling sighs, the courtyard's clinging gloom retreated like melting snow - silent yet swift. *Ding-ling!*

The clear chime shattered the silence. Dolls hanging under the eaves ceased their mourning, bursting with radiance that pierced through the haze. Long-absent sunlight cascaded down, making the Courtyard's white gravel glitter like stars. Beside the grave-like leaf pile, the wild sprout stood tall in golden light.

As if the sorrow remained, yet had gently dissolved into this brilliance. "Thank you..."

The old man stroked the dolls, his voice still hoarse, but warmth had returned to his palms.


When Hiyoribou left the Courtyard, she still carried several dolls gifted by the old man in her arms.

The dolls were exquisite and lovely, each wearing bright smiles that seemed to radiate warmth simply by being observed.

The old man escorted her to the gate, his perpetually worried face now softened with a gentle, serene smile.

His hands-warm and calloused from years of woodcarving-held hers as he repeatedly urged her to take good care of herself.

Sensing his reluctance to part, Hiyoribou grasped his hand and beamed back at him.

"I'll bring these dolls to Silence Forest... Kodama will be delighted! When his spiritual power Recovers fully, I'll bring him here to visit you."

At their final farewell, she glanced back at the Courtyard where dolls and sunny dolls twirled in the Wind, dancing beneath the eaves as if basking in sunlight.

Gazing at the strange yet beautiful scene beneath the roof, she murmured softly:

"Sadness and loneliness are both part of life."

"Just as light and shadow always coexist, inseparable companions."

"Yet even so..."

"As long as the light in our hearts remains unextinguished... The skies will always clear!"

This is the Talisman Pass for December. The focus of this event was Taki.

The limited rewards for the November Talisman Pass are the Golden Navigator skin, Sand Sailor illustration, Adventurer illustration and the Golden Sailing frame.


Tagitsuhime received another batch of letters from her friends in Heian-Kyo.

She held the letters high, excitedly sharing the news with Shiro-uneri. When Taki walked in, she was swinging her legs, and those curious eyes looking over were even brighter than gold in the sunlight.

"Let's go!" Tagitsuhime jumped down from the high stool, lifted her chin, and tugged at Taki's sleeve. "The Sand Sea-I haven't seen it yet! Taki, you want to see it too, right? They say it is a vast expanse of gold! I think gold is so beautiful-it must be a sea made of golden dust! Auctopus would love it! Should we sail by the Golden Tentacles?"

Taki turned to Shiro-uneri with a helpless smile. "Who told her that?" 

However powerful the Golden Tentacles is, it can't sail into the desert. Besides, sand isn't gold-it has no value. That's not something you'd like, Sharkie."

"Oh, come on-" Tagitsuhime pouted. "I think I would really like it!"

Shiro-uneri smiled gently, with a hint of mischief.

"Why not head in that direction?" The ship's doctor spoke softly, quieting > Taki's protest before it could be voiced. "Kanihime told her. She is Sharkie's new friend."

"She said if you keep heading west, crossing waves as fierce as 'killer- waves, you might see such a spectacle-maybe a mirage, maybe an illusion, where the sea turns to sand and the ship can only travel through the desert."

"Maybe, deep within the sands, there could even be gold hidden."

"I am just as curious. Why don't we go see for ourselves? After all, the Golden Tentacles is always moving forward in search and adventure."

"You wouldn't shy away from an adventure like this. Or is it, Taki, that you have some bad memories of the desert?"


Although he had always sailed the seas, Taki had seen the "desert" before. That memory wasn't exactly terrible, but it was far from pleasant. He had stuffed it carelessly into some corner of his mind, only for it to resurface now.

When he was younger, he traveled far with his father's merchant ship. To open up new trade routes, the ship docked along the coast, becoming a caravan that stretched onto land. After a long journey, Taki saw the real "sea of sand" for the first time-a dazzling, vast expanse of gold, utterly different from the ocean.

The dry wind made him uncomfortable, so Taki had to pull up his hood and change his clothes. His father's business negotiations did not go smoothly, but the young Taki, unlike his usual self, neither joined in nor spoke much.

From the moment he stepped into the desert, his voice inexplicably went hoarse, as if the ever-present fine sand had secretly filled his throat. When no one was around, Taki crouched down to talk to a lizard crawling on the sand, but even he couldn't recognize his own voice. It was dry and grating, and naturally, he couldn't work his usual silver tongue during the negotiations.

The young Taki tried to shrink into the shadows of this unfamiliar land, but there was always someone determined to drag him out, like pulling a fish from the sea and tossing it under the blazing sun.

"I have indeed heard of Young Master Taki," the desert lord looked down at him. "They say you are wise beyond your years. with remarkable insight...Everyone who works with you sings your praises. But here, Young Master Taki, why are you so silent?"

Taki remained silent. His father smiled and explained, "It's Taki's first time in the desert-perhaps he's dazzled by the sights here."

But the desert lord pressed on, "Still, I would love to see the Young Master's skill in appraising treasures. Have you heard the tales? This place is also called the 'Golden Sand Sea'-countless people have braved the heat waves, hoping to find real gold and precious treasures buried in the sand. Sadly, few have succeeded. But if it were Young Master Taki with the sharp-eye, what would happen then?"

...But in the end, his father's negotiations finished, and the caravan decided to return. The endless golden expanse nearly burned Taki's eyes, and the heat of the sand almost scorched his hands. The young Taki found neither the so-called "real gold" nor the "treasure he wanted."

His father sighed, "Taki. The prerequisite for searching... is that you have something you truly want."


In the years that followed, he would often envision that dazzling, blinding expanse of golden light.

The fleeting memories of the sand were buried early on-painless, inconsequential, seemingly without impact; yet precisely because they became part of Taki's life so soon, they fused into the very core of who he was.

Like a fleeting glimpse of light, this past had already foreshadowed his fate while he was still a boy. It silently declared that, whether as a human or later as a fallen spirit, Taki was like a solitary vessel drifting through the desolate, silent sea of sand-barren, vast, endless yellow sand, all of it utterly worthless to him.

The Golden Tentacles always sailed upon the open sea. They braved surging giant waves, searching for lost treasures swallowed by nature or guarded by sea monsters, believing these would fill the emptiness within. The ship gleamed with gold and splendor, yet coins and treasures were carelessly piled in unremarkable corners. After tasting the thrill of life and death, Taki found himself bored, and so conceived the idea of hosting the Eve of the Golden Dawn.

Sometimes, Taki felt that nothing had truly changed from the past.

He smiled at every guest who arrived at the Eve of the Golden Dawn, watching them vie for their coveted treasures, letting them trade all manner of things-valuable or worthless alike.

This, too, was a kind of "trade." Yet whether he gave or received, it all seemed to hold little meaning for Taki.

One guest asked, "But as the first mate of the Golden Tentacles, a ship that lacks for nothing, as someone in charge, is there truly nothing you desire, Mr. Taki?"

"Ah." Taki replied with a light smile, tapping his lips with a fingertip, "That, is a question I should be asking you, isn't it?"

Yet sometimes, Taki would wonder.

What is it that I truly want...?

Once again, he envisioned that dry, golden expanse. In a daze, he wondered: the treasure buried in the sand, the 'real' gold beneath the grains...what was it, really?


At this moment, in the sun-drenched cabin of the Golden Tentacles, Taki met Shiro-uneri's gentle gaze with quiet composure, his own eyes betraying nothing.

"Let's go then," he said, his tone with little hint of emotion. "After all, it's an adventure-hope it won't let me down too much... And you, Shiro-uneri, what are you speculating about me in front of Sharkie?"

"Oh," Shiro-uneri lowered his eyes, but the smile lingered at his lips. "You caught me. Well, whether it's the 'curse' or your past... Taki, you've always been so mysterious."

The Golden Tentacles set out again, skillfully weaving through the giant waves and sailing beneath the looming claws of sea monsters. When sunlight once more spilled across the sea, they arrived at the legendary Mirage Sand Sea.

Tagitsuhime rushed onto the deck. "Shiro-uneri! Taki! Look! We're here!" -It was a dazzling gold, utterly unlike anything in Taki's memories.

Golden sunlight bathed the sea. Surrounded by giant waves, this "Sand Sea" truly resembled a mirage conjured by dreams. The Golden Tentacles broke through the waves, sailing across the boundless "desert," the whole world ablaze with brilliant gold. Auctopus waved its tentacles, Tagitsuhime reached out to touch the "seawater" swirling on the deck-water that flowed like fine sand through her fingers, swept away by the damp wind.

"It's nothing like what Taki said!" Tagitsuhime laughed joyfully. "I really love it! It's so golden-just like real gold!"

Taki replied, "But sand is still just sand. It's not treasure, but something without value."

"What does it matter? I like carving with gold simply because it's so shiny! And now-"

Tagitsuhime spread her hands, beaming, "There's such a vast golden land! It is what I love-so it's treasure to me!"

Taki was taken aback.

Shiro-uneri chuckled softly, "Sharkie's eye for treasure is not bad either... right, Taki?"

"Ah." After a moment, Taki laughed quietly. "In terms of this, I should learn from Sharkie."

The Golden Tentacles broke through the waves.

Hizz climbed up the mast. From above, the Golden Tentacles sailing through the Mirage Sand Sea looked itself like a precious nugget of true gold.

How does one find the treasures buried in the sand?

Taki always thought it took unwavering patience, wits, sharp sight... and a bit of smooth talking, along with every adventurer's skill.

But he never expected that-

Sometimes, it just took a bit of genuine sincerity. The treasure he sought had always been right by his side.

This is the Talisman Pass for January. The focus of this event was Ryukaku.

The limited rewards for the January Talisman Pass are the Petalhorn Grace skin, Kite's Wishes illustration, Loong's Retreat illustration and Blossom Spring frame.


A pear blossom petal drifted lightly and swayingly into Loongjue's palm, and she knew that spring had quietly arrived once again in Yunhua City.

She sat on the rooftop, gazing downward. The entire city was immersed in a warm sea of flowers. With each gust of wind, the floral fragrance and the hum of voices rose together to the eaves. The doors of the martial training hall stood wide open, where Yu was earnestly leading disciples through their fist techniques. Beneath the shaded corridors, neighbors who had finished their practice set up a small table, gathered around it, debating over a game of chess. Further away, beyond the clusters of pink-and-white blossoming trees resembling clouds, a few kites swayed lazily in the eastern breeze, while children's laughter carried from afar.

Loongjue gently closed her eyes as sunlight seeped through leaves onto her face. She felt time slipping quietly through the alleys and rooftops, imbuing even her with the soft, joyful warmth of spring.

"I knew I'd find you here," Xuanhe's voice suddenly came from beside her. "Sister, you're just like the old days-always lazing around in places like this. If you really have so much time, you should come help me at the Chief's Residence."

"Mmm." Loongjue chuckled. "Aren't you too lazing around right now?"

Shoulder to shoulder, heads leaning together, they sat quietly for a while in the peaceful spring breeze.

"I remember when Yunhua City didn't have so many flowers blooming," said Loongjue.

"Right." Xuanhe followed Loongjue's gaze toward the flourishing blossoms across the city. Slightly surprised, her eyes piercing through the pink-white mist as if looking back at the path they had traveled.

"It's been a long time. Bit by bit, these flowers were planted by everyone across Yunhua City over these years. Before...some were burned, others withered. But more and more people began planting them, and with this year's abundant rain... Despite past misfortunes, these flowers bloom even more vibrantly than before."

As she spoke, Xuanhe turned to look at Loongjue. The shadows cast by those flowers trembled against Loongjue's clothes, and stray petals drifted onto her hair. Her eyes shone bright, reflecting the gentle sunlight and the city's swaying blossoms.

"You love these flowers, don't you?"

Xuanhe's eyes curved into a shrewdly yet certain smile.

"Isn't it incredible? The people of Yunhua City did this together without planning it. Care to guess why, sister?"



As a child, Xuanhe often wore such a shrewd smile.

Back then, mother held Xuanhe's hand, leading her through the drooping branches of crabapple blossoms, bringing her to Loongjue on a similarly radiant spring day. Xuanhe's smile didn't hold the certainty and serenity it does now. She masked her insecurity with shrewdness, probing for Loongjue's thoughts indirectly with all sorts of questions.

She would ask bluntly, "Will we be together forever?" Or guess shrewdly, "Sister, you don't like that bully on West Street, right? He thinks he can bully others just because he's got some strength. Do you want me to teach him a lesson?" And there were countless other subtle inquiries-"Do you prefer crabapple or peony flowers, Sis?" or "Sister, you like lighter sweetness in your dessert, right?"

Through these exchanges, they gradually developed an understanding. Only after a long period of time together did Xuanhe come to realize that with Loongjue, acceptance wasn't a multiple-choice question with only one right

answer.

Loongjue said, "You don't need to guess or cater to my preferences. What matters isn't the choice itself—it's you."

Xuanhe, unusually bewildered, asked, "Me?"

Loongjue replied, "You are Xuanhe, my little sister. So, whatever you decide or choose, it's fine by me."

After a long silence, Xuanhe hesitantly asked, "...Really? As long as it's... no matter what I do, even if I make mistakes, you won't leave me behind, Sister?"

Loongjue nodded. "Of course I won't leave you." Then she hastily added, "But if you do make a mistake... we'll have to apologize and make amends together."

Xuanhe blinked, then laughed out loud.

From then on, she no longer needed to hide her emotions with something else-she was shrewd and smart by nature, and she was free of insecurity. She still loved asking all kinds of questions, but she asked with unwavering confidence, and her questions were more of a triumphant declaration-"I was right about it!" "That's why I know my sister best!"

This was the Xuanhe who had fully bloomed like a flower.

It was also then that Loongjue finally understood her mother's words.

"See? You're doing just fine. Guiding someone is like tending to flowers-it takes immense time and patience. But first-" Yuxiang smiled.

"You must plant the seed."


So... Xuanhe's question isn't difficult to answer either.

The Wind carried the kites into the higher azure sky, while petals swirling in the air. A few spring swallows sliced through the heavens, parting the drifting clouds as they bathed in the gentle sunlight, vanishing into the distant expanse of green mountains.

The debate over the chess game under the corridor shade gradually shifted into neighborhood small talk. Yu wiped sweat from her brow, straightened her back amidst the crowd's praises, and strode proudly out of the martial hall's gate.

Loongjue listened to these chaotic yet heartwarming bustles, and she smiled. "Naturally, it's because of... hope."

Seeds of hope, seeds of the good-even though Yunhua City has endured misfortune, people still believe the future can improve and are willing to act to make it so.

Human hearts yearn for good things, for warmth, and for spring.

And thus, spring arrives.

Xuanhe chuckled, "If your star pupil Yu were to answer, she might phrase it more precisely."

-So, what would be the reason?

When the Qing Loong soared past the clouds, it planted that very seed long, long ago-for Guangxia, and for Yunhua City.

Good and evil will always meet their reckoning. And the life-nurturing rain will eventually come.

But...it isn't solely because of the Qing Loong recorded in the Book of Yunhua.

Xuanhe tilted her head to study Loongjue again. She thought, yes-back then, it was precisely because of that unshakable confidence. Even when she and her sister walked opposing paths, she believed they would ultimately

converge.

Surely, the hope in the hearts of everyone at the Loongbreath and in the city, took root, sprouted, and blossomed the same way-nurtured by the spring rains.


Not far away, children flying kites called out loudly, "Director! Our kite's stuck in the tree-could you help us?"

The neighbors watching the chess game also thought of her and chimed in with laughter, "Right! The Loongbreath Director must know the solution to that move. Why not let her settle this?"

Yu stood beneath the tree, hands cupped around her mouth, her gaze clear and posture relaxed, without a trace of the timid shyness from their first meeting. She shouted cheerfully, "Master! I've finished guiding them through their lessons! What about-we-fly a kite-together? I made a Qing Loong kite-a giant one!"

Loongjue stood up with effortless grace, stretching lazily under the dappled spring sunlight.

The wind tousled her hair as petals drifted around them, and Loongjue turned back to flash Xuanhe a radiant smile.

"See? I can be busy. Seems like I can't go help you at the Chief's Residence now."

Xuanhe rested her chin in her hand and smiled back.

For a moment, she had so much to say. She wanted to say, Yes, Sister, you belong here-amid sunlit streets and bustling crowds, surrounded by these small but meaningful needs.

You're not like those deities seated above the clouds. You're the sparrow that carries the light of spring, diving gracefully yet resolutely into the mortal. world, sowing seeds and leading me into this sea of blooming flowers.

But in the end, all her words dissolved into the warm breeze sweeping across the sky, gently shaking loose petals and carrying away Xuanhe's unspoken blessings.

Xuanhe only murmured softly, "Go ahead, Sister."

And just as Xuanhe had imagined-like a spring swallow taking flight-Loongjue leaped gracefully from the rooftop, descending into a courtyard awash with floral hues, immersing into the vibrant tapestry of mortal life.

This is the Talisman Pass for February. The focus of this event was Shiki.

The limited rewards for the January Talisman Pass are the Ginkgo Gliding skin, Autumn Gingko illustration, and Ginkgo Deerhop frame.


To Shiki,


This marks the fourth autumn since you left, and the seasons have begun to cycle normally again.

I’ve traveled to many places, I climbed Mt. Enken once more, where we spent countless cycles together, passed by fields now heavy with ripening rice, and lingered by ponds blooming with summer lotuses…

My footsteps have nearly covered the entire Land of Spring’s Lament. I’ve witnessed plants wither and flourish with the heavens’ rhythm, and people farming in harmony with the seasons. When spring recedes, cherry blossoms scatter from branches as lush greenery swells, almost dripping with emerald vitality. As summer departs, cicada songs fade while the air carries the warm fragrance of grains and fruits. No one needs to pay a price to sustain this cycle anymore-and you, at last, no longer have to guard the seasons by forgetting everything.

Everything is so peaceful, so ordinary…these were scenes I-the former Mamoru-never dared to hope for.

I returned to the Seasons Village. That mountainside hamlet is now embraced by vast clusters of golden ginkgo leaves, their veins seemingly saturated with sunlight glowing so brightly it stings the eyes. Amid those radiant shadows, Grandma Eba-do you still remember her? The Leaffolk elder who fed us, then quietly left to protect the village in the end…the one we met during our final journey together, who weighed heavy on our hearts yet filled them with warmth.

She took my hand, weeping uncontrollably. She asked about you…about your whereabouts, pleading to know when you might return. You who brought the seasons back to us-how long must we wait for you to revisit the village and see this prosperous world?

She said, “My time grows short…while I can still move, I gathered letters from everyone to the Goddess of Seasons. Could you…promise to deliver them if she returns?”

Those letters were as numerous as swirling ginkgo leaves-and just as laden with longing.

Like the “Shiki and Mamoru’s Little Adventure” we once wrote together…perhaps these too can preserve moments you missed or forgot. I want to tell you everything-every path I walked, every sight I saw. As if you never left, as if we still journey side by side.

So when the autumn breeze brushed through my hair again, I picked up my pen to write you this letter.


Mamoru


Goddess of the Seasons,


I wonder if you would still remember me by then... I am Eba, the little girl who always followed Granny around in the village back then.

After learning about the ritual of seasonal transition... Over these years, I have been studying the ancient texts preserved in our village. The Seasons Village has always worshipped the Goddess of the Seasons, and I sought to understand you better. Not just as the so-called deity, nor merely through our brief encounter—I wanted to know your past, to rediscover the forgotten aspects of you.

Then, in a tattered woodcutter's journal, I came across a story about autumn-

autumn-

It must have been an autumn long ago. A lost woodcutter strayed deep into the mountains. Amidst the golden ginkgo leaves, he met you.

There you were, slumbering among the golden ginkgo leaves of autumn, radiant like the warm sun.

Perhaps it was the woodcutter's footsteps that disturbed your rest, or maybe the sweet aroma of the dried persimmons in his arms awakened you-you opened your eyes.

"You came from the foot of the mountain! Such aroma... Is it dessert? It smells so good! Did you bring this from below? Mr. Protector doesn't like to talk about the world down there with me... Autumn here in the mountains is especially beautiful! But what is autumn like...beneath the mountains? Could you tell me about it?”

Though the woodcutter spoke only of ordinary things, the busy harvest, festival drums, the scent of roasted sweet potatoes—you listened with such fascination. Before parting, you summoned a golden flying bird to guide him through the twilight.

In the journal's final entry, the woodcutter wrote, "I think I met the deity of autumn. She seemed so drawn to the liveliness of the mortal world, yet she was left alone to guard these mountain woods. If I ever see her again, I'll bring not just dried persimmons... and if possible, invite her to the village festival."

Sadly, that promise was never fulfilled

Now, your guidance...all you have given has brought the seasons back to us and returned my beloved Granny to the village. The cruel tradition of abandoning the elderly for survival is gone…everything is getting better. 

Every autumn festival, we prepare the sweetest persimmons, the most fragrant sweet potatoes, and even the liveliest celebrations.

If you ever return…would you come and see us again?

All of us wish to say thank you to you.

Granny can no longer write. But we all remember you-and always will.


With deepest respect, Eba.


Lady Shiki,


As the lineage of the Leaffolk Lord that maintains the existence of the Land of Spring's Lament, we once committed an unforgivable sin. Even as I write this letter, I am still filled with deep, deep remorse.

My mother was the chronicler who recorded the Pact of the Seasons, ritual procedures, and the deeds of past Mamoru for the Leaffolk Lord. In my memory, she was always stern, serious, and cold. From childhood, she made me witness every seasonal transition ritual, forcing me to record everything with cold detachment. She said records should inherently be emotionless-only by stripping away feelings could the world remain free from the corruption of the evil deity and function normally. How cruel, my mother.

I could neither accept her methods nor the old traditions upheld by the lord's lineage. I tried to escape her countless times in those years, to break free from her control and that suffocating duty... It wasn't until the evil deity awakened, until the world collapsed, until you sacrificed yourself to restore the seasons, that I finally found the courage to stand before her and bid a proper farewell.

Mother had aged significantly. Hunched over, she quietly fixed her gaze upon me. I expected scolding or reprimands... but instead, she sighed deeply and placed her hand on my head.

"Go. Right and wrong can only be understood through your own experience. Perhaps we had other choices back then, perhaps not... but now, this path is yours to walk." Her voice carried a gentleness and weariness I'd never heard before. "Though we've had our differences... we're like the diverging veins of the same ginkgo leaf. Only through parting... can new life begin."

And so, I embarked on my own journey. I still keep records, but no longer of the so-called Pact of the Seasons. Instead, I document the tender green of rice fields swaying during Grain Rain, the returning swallows skimming rivers with fresh mud in spring, how children grow up and utter their first "Mama"...

I believe that long ago, before everything went awry, we were meant to record the vibrant, ever-changing seasons themselves. In just a few years, my compiled history of the Land of Spring's Lament has grown thick. If you return... I wish to apologize, to atone for all those in my bloodline who failed you... and to personally deliver this true history of the seasons themselves into your hands.

I want you to know-this land has finally relearned how to breathe, because of you.


Sincerely, Chiyoha


Mamoru carefully sorted the weighty letters one by one and solemnly

placed them into the wooden box.

He shouldered his pack, ready to continue his journey.

The gentle, soothing autumn wind brushed against his face once more. He caught the fragrance of ripened fruit and the scent of dissipating hearth smoke. From beyond the field ridges came the laughter of children at play, occasionally mingled with mothers' calls for them to return home. This was a mortal world no longer bound by pacts or troubled by malevolent gods—a world where everyone could live as themselves, peacefully passing each ordinary day.

Rustling ginkgo leaves, blown by the wind, lightly settled on his shoulder.

Sensing something, Mamoru turned to look back.

The grove of ginkgo trees swayed in the wind, their golden leaves shimmering as sunlight filtered through the branches. For a fleeting moment, he thought he saw Shiki. Countless golden ginkgo leaves formed the hem of her robe, tracing the faint outline of her figure. She seemed to be sleeping deep within that radiant light, nestled peacefully between the branches and the autumn breeze.

Letters from all around the entire Land of Spring's Lament-along with the thoughts of its people-transformed into golden flying birds fluttering their wings, and into foxes and deer dashing through the woods, all chasing after that silhouette.


Mamoru smiled faintly.


"When will we meet again?" He murmured softly, "I always see you in the changing seasons. But you as Shiki... just as Shiki-where must I go to find the real you?”


Bathed in the golden glow of the setting sun, Mamoru turned back to his planned direction-toward the next rotating season-and continued his unending pursuit.
